ParlaMint-DK_20171012100222	The debate is open. The first to speak is the rapporteur for the Social Democratic Party, Mr President. Male Larsen. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012100244	Thank you. This bill concerns part of the financing of the agreement that is behind a new renewable energy support model, an agreement that has been concluded between the government and the Danish People's Party for approx. 14 days ago. Overall, I regret that there is no higher level of ambition for the Danish expansion of VE than is seen in that proposal. Having said that, I would like to deal with L 39. Let me start by saying that the Social Democracy agrees with what is described as the overall intention of the draft law, namely to make it clearer in Danish legislation when the aid scheme expires because of EU state aid rules. The greatest possible transparency of legislation can only be beneficial to all parties. At the same time, the Social Democratic Party supports the part of the bill in which it is proposed to remove the solar pool for 2017 and replace it with a technology-specific tender for small solar installations. This is a direct expiry of an agreement concluded by the Energy Regulatory Group. By converting the pot into a supply we get much more green energy for the money. The supply is expected to provide an expansion that could deliver 1 year of power consumption to approx. 12,500 Danish households. However, we note that in the hearings there is a particularly important criticism to which we can subscribe, namely that, for example, there is a lack of transparency on the part of the Commission. Danish Construction demands an overall strategy in the solar field, so that once and for all, clarity is created as to how solar cells are promoted most appropriately. We've asked for that before, and I'll be happy to do it again. However, here too, our immediate support for the draft law, which covers a great many different matters, and let me just mention two of the points where we are sceptical. First of all, in relation to the Green Scheme, which the government will not continue when the current state aid authorisation expires. There is little support for this decision in the hearings. Let me just mention some of the critics who are Randers, Jammerbugt, Vesthimmerland, Skive, Aalborg and Herning municipalities. Agriculture & Food criticized stopped, and KL criticized stopped. Everyone finds the abolition of the system problematic. In particular, it is of course worrying that several municipalities describe how current projects are already in process getting into a mess in the municipalities. Secondly, we take note of the criticism of the downgrading of the balance-sheet allowance, which is also to be found in the consultation responses. For example: Danish Energy states that they consider it highly questionable that for the third time within two years the authorities consider it necessary to adjust the scheme. This creates uncertainty in the market and doubts about future investors, a development that is in no way beneficial to the green transition. We need stable framework conditions so that we can secure the lowest possible prices for renewable energy in the future. The Social Democratic Party therefore considers it appropriate to ask further questions about this before we can possibly support the proposal. We look forward to this in committee. We also find it worrying that the RE model is being changed in a way that the industry is opposed to. It will be significantly more expensive than necessary, and as the Green Scheme disappears, there will be greater concern among the local population and resistance to having wind turbines set up, for example. In the light of all the above, and since the draft law covers a number of different proposals that we consider necessary to deal with individually, the Social Democrats are proposing to split up this bill. It should be noted that we expect a number of uncertainties to be clarified in the committee's proceedings.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012100640	Thank you. There are no brief comments. Then we move on to the next rapporteur, and that is Mr. Mikkel Dencker from the Danish People's Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012100654	Thank you, sir. President. The draft law here, L 39, as the previous rapporteur on the floor mentioned, contains a large number of different elements, all of which have a common feature, that they fall within the scope of energy legislation, but that is also where the similarities first and foremost end. When you take some of the various elements and review them, there is, of course, the element that the expiry of certain aid schemes, where the expiry date is not indicated today by the law, but where you have to look elsewhere for when the aid authorisation from the EU system expires. In this way, it is the one that governs the expiry date of the aid scheme, and there we think it is sensible and educational that things should be written into the legislation so that it is not unnecessarily difficult to find out when an aid scheme expires. In the same way, we also think that it is sensible to allow the temporary operation of power generation facilities without having a normal power generation permit. It is, of course, only in exceptional cases that such a system should be possible, but it is sensible to have it, because if it is necessary. Similarly, I would also like to say about the element that deadlines for grid connection and application are being set for the payment of increased price surcharges for the series of transitional arrangements made in 2013 for solar installations, that it is now more than four years since the scheme was adopted by the Folketing, and then it must also be reasonable that an expiry date should be set for the application of this scheme. So these are also elements that the Danish People's Party can be involved in. In addition, there is a provision on the solar pool for 2017. It is a decision to change the pool adopted by the Energy Association in 2017, which is part of the Danish People's Party, which is why we also support that element. So overall I can say that it is a good deal of mixed elements in the energy sector, but these are all elements that the Danish People's Party can endorse, so we support the bill.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012100928	Thank you, sir. Mikkel Dencker. There are also no brief comments, so we move quickly on to Mrs Marianne Bredal from the Left, the Danish Liberal Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012100949	Thank you. With Bill L 39 we propose to clarify the expiry date of a number of aid schemes under the VE Act covered by the EU State aid rules. In this way, the draft law aims to standardise the aid schemes provided for in the VE Act in relation to the EU legal basis for State aid rules. The draft law also proposes to include a pool of 1 MW in some of the aid schemes for smaller renewable plants. This will ensure that there is no unforeseen and uncontrollable expansion of aid schemes. In the government we fight for as much green conversion as possible for the money. Therefore, the draft law contains a further proposal to remove the solar pool for 2017 in favour of a more cost-effective technology-specific offer for solar installations below 1 MW. By allowing the solar projects to bid in each of them, the project simply delivers the most green energy at the best price. The draft law also proposes the possibility of temporary operation of power generation facilities at sea without any power generation permit. It is appropriate in the case of an unfulfilled mortgage holder taking over and operating the facility for a limited period of time. Last but not least, it is proposed that the balance sheet remuneration should be adjusted downwards in order to ensure consistency with the European Commission's approval of the scheme. All in all, the bill thus supports the government's clear ambition for a cost-effective renewable energy expansion. That is why, of course, the Left supports L 39.

I fear that if we adopt what is here, it will result in more of the existing wind turbines being taken down. I think it is a pity that we have ended up in this, because this agreement on the technology-neutral supply could well be imagined that the government would like to be reflected into a future energy agreement. There I think that in a future energy agreement, tools should be chosen to ensure the maximum possible amount of renewable energy for the money, and that is not what has been proposed here.

ParlaMint-DK_20171012103703	Thank you, sir. Orla Østerby. And then the trip has come to the Minister of Energy, Supply and Climate.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012103710	Thank you for that, President. I would like to start by thanking the rapporteurs for their comments on the draft law. The main measure in the bill is that the solar pool for 2017 is proposed to be repealed. The solar pool was decided in 2013, but we have come to the conclusion in the conciliation circle that the funds can be used more efficiently by implementing a technology-specific PV supply instead. Therefore, the bill implies that we cancel the payout of the solar pool in 2017. A separate bill on the implementation of the technology-specific PV supply will be submitted next year. The reason why we will replace the solar pool in 2017 with a technology-specific offer in 2018 for solar installations below 1 MW is that we estimate that we can thus get more renewable energy for the money. This part of the bill was submitted in consultation with the draft law. The late consultation is due to the fact that the termination of the pool was only agreed shortly before the draft law was submitted. The consultation note has therefore only been sent to the Danish Parliament yesterday. In addition, a number of more technical elements are included in the draft law, which is essentially to exercise due diligence. As has already been shown, the draft law is, I believe, one of those which can be called a genuine joint legislative proposal. In short, in addition to removing the solar pool, the purpose is that we will insert expiry dates into the VE Act in accordance with the State legal basis for a number of aid schemes. This brings our legislation fully into line with the expiry of aid schemes in the European Union. We will downgrade the balance sheet remuneration to ensure that we do not make overcompensation, which is an EU requirement. We will set a ceiling of 1 MW in a number of aid schemes for small renewable energy installations to ensure that the schemes do not run out of control. We will include grid connection deadlines and deadline for application for payment in a number of support schemes for solar installations from 2013. This makes it clear how many plants are included in old transitional arrangements. Finally, we will allow for the temporary operation of power generation facilities at sea without a power generation permit to ensure that offshore wind farms can also be operated in special cases without a power generation permit. The bill and related notices are planned to enter into force on 1 January 2018. I welcome the interest expressed in the draft law here in Things today. I look forward to continuing the discussion on the individual elements of the draft law in the Committee on Energy, Supply and Climate Affairs. And I would like to thank you for your comments and would like to ask for a favourable treatment of the draft law.

ParlaMint-DK_20171012110535	The debate is open. The first speaker is the rapporteur of the Socialist Group in the European Parliament, Mr President. Nick Hækkerup. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012110559	Thank you. The bill we are now considering will allow foreign, security and defence policy considerations to be included in Denmark's decision to allow electricity or gas pipelines to be installed on Danish territorial waters. Technically, the bill is made in such a way that it happens by the Minister for Foreign Affairs giving an opinion to the Minister for Energy, Supply and Climate, whom he or she then has to accept. What the Foreign Minister is basing that attitude on is, as the bill says, a free political judgement, which can include everything that can be seen as relevant to foreign, security or defence policy in the broadest sense, which, to put it mildly, is not so little. The recommendation will not indicate what considerations the Foreign Minister has made, but there will only be a yes or a no to whether the location of the installation in Danish territorial waters is compatible with our foreign, security and defence policy interests and concerns. Of course, in the practical execution of this, it will be seen as relatively closed, but since there will typically be consideration for other states involved, it is relevant and not unlike what we do in those situations where there are other states involved or where there are foreign policy considerations, for example. as you know from the work of the Foreign Policy Name etc. The Act is set to enter into force on 1 January 2018, but will also apply to cases pending at the time of the entry into force of the Act. This means that if an application has been submitted before the law enters into force, but the application has not been finalised, that application will be subject to the law. In the comments to the bill it is called the general starting point. It's enough to tighten it. It is undoubtedly not contrary to the basic law to create the structure that has been created here, but there will of course always be reason to be cautious in applying laws to facts which are prior to the adoption, confirmation and publication of the law, because that is the basis for citizens to know what legal status they have to comply with, that is to say, the legal situation is that which is applied at the time the facts are there. However, when this happens in this case, it is because of course the law is generally formulated, but designed to apply to the so-called North Stream 2 gas pipeline, which is also abundantly demonstrated by the consultation answers that have been received. For the Social Democratic Party it is obvious that when electricity and gas pipelines are to be built in Danish territorial waters, they may have geopolitical interests, and when they do, it should of course also be assessed in foreign, security and defence policy, and it is also obvious that this applies to the North Stream 2 project, which is why the Social Democratic Party can support the bill in its entirety.

ParlaMint-DK_20171012111415	Thank you to the rapporteur. The next rapporteur is Hans Andersen from the Left. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012111422	Thank you. This bill stems from the desire to include security policy, foreign policy and defence policy interests when permission is given to dismantle power cables and pipelines for the transport of hydrocarbons on the Danish territorial sea. Today, there is no clear legal basis for including these considerations in the decision on who is to be authorised. The draft law therefore calls for the administrative processing of applications for the decommissioning of pipelines in the territorial sea to take place in two stages in the future. At the first stage, the Minister for Foreign Affairs shall draw up a recommendation to include foreign, security and defence policy considerations, which shall be taken into account in the decision of the Minister for Energy and, if the recommendation is positive, a usual environmental and security assessment shall be made at the second stage of the application concerned. If, on the other hand, the recommendation considers that conditions are not compatible with foreign and security policy interests, applicants shall be refused. This bill also contains a number of other elements that support the central purpose of the bill. I would like to touch briefly on the fact that the draft law also provides for the possibility of charging for the processing of applications for decommissioning in the maritime territory. It is essential that there is an opportunity to take wider security policy considerations into account in decisions on these activities in Danish territorial waters. This must, of course, be done on the basis of legislation, and the draft law provides the government with a clear legal basis for protecting the foreign, security and defence interests of the empire in connection with the dismantling of power cables and pipelines. In the future, there will therefore be certainty that activities are fully compatible with Danish interests. Against this background, the Left supports this bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20171012113151	Thank you. The essential element of the draft law is therefore that a new provision is proposed in the continental shelf law, Paragraph 3a, which regulates the right to be allowed to shut down power cables and pipelines for the transport of hydrocarbons in the territorial sea. The effect of the proposed amendment is to introduce a new stage in the assessment of applications for the decommissioning of pipelines in the territorial sea. ParlaMint-DK_20171012113608	Thank you to the rapporteur. I shall now give the floor to the Minister for Energy, Supply and Climate. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012113615	Thank you for that, President, and thank the rapporteurs for their comments on the draft law. As has been shown, the bill is the result of a political desire to be able to take into account the interests of the kingdom's foreign, security and defence policy in relation to applications for the decommissioning of power cables and pipelines for the transport of hydrocarbons on the Danish territorial sea. The reason for this is that, in the context of a specific application, the government has become aware that there is no clear legal basis for including such broader considerations in the current legal basis. The main element of the draft law is therefore that a new provision is proposed in Section 3a of the continental shelf Act, which regulates the right to be permitted to dismantle power cables and pipelines for the transport of hydrocarbons in the maritime territory. According to the proposed section 3a, authorisation for decommissioning in the territorial sea can only be granted if it is compatible with the foreign, security and defence policy interests of the Kingdom. Until now, the establishment and operation of power lines and pipelines for the transport of hydrocarbons in the maritime territory has been regulated in particular by a decree issued under the State's law of the territorial sea. However, the Order does not provide a clear legal basis for the inclusion of wider foreign, security and defence policy considerations. The proposed amendments relate only to the territorial sea, i.e. that part of the waters extending from the coast to 12 nautical miles, corresponding to approx. Twenty-two kilometers. Denmark's international obligations under the Convention on the Law of the Sea prevent foreign, security and defence policy considerations from being involved in relation to applications on the continental shelf outside the territorial sea. It is proposed that the draft law enters into force on 1 January 2018 and that the new rules, in accordance with normal practice, will apply to applications which have not been finalised at the time of entry into force. The consequences of the proposed changes are the introduction of a new stage in the assessment of applications for decommissioning of pipelines on the maritime territory. At the new first stage, it must be assessed whether the project in question is compatible with the foreign, security and defence policy interests of the empire. The Minister for Energy, Supply and Climate will obtain a recommendation from the Minister for Foreign Affairs, which will involve other ministers as appropriate, in particular the Minister for Justice, the Minister for Defence and the Minister for Energy, Supply and Climate. The recommendation must be based on the decision of the case, i.e. the recommendation is binding. If the setting is negative, a refusal shall be notified. If the recommendation is positive, the authorities shall carry out the usual environmental and safety assessment corresponding to the applicable scheme. The proposal authorises the Minister for Energy, Supply and Climate to lay down detailed rules for the administrative processing of applications in the territorial sea. A number of minor amendments to the law are also proposed. Among other things, it has. to support the processing of applications and to clarify the administrative framework for the processing of applications. Applicants and owners of pipeline installations in the territorial sea shall be required to provide information to the Minister. If the obligation to provide information is not complied with, fines may be imposed. Furthermore, it is established that refusals based on the foreign, security and defence interests of the Kingdom cannot be brought before the Energy Appeals Board. Refugees may be brought before the courts by suing the Minister for Energy, Supply and Climate within 6 months of refusal. In addition, the draft law contains a number of technical amendments, including: It is proposed that rules should be laid down on the collection of payments for the processing of applications for the decommissioning of pipeline installations in the maritime territory. Several of the provisions of the draft law are applicable since they already result from existing regulations issued in respect of the territorial sea on the basis of the State Supreme Court. It was considered appropriate on this occasion to establish the rules of law in parallel with those of the continental shelf outside the territorial sea. I am pleased with the interest that Things have expressed today in the draft law. I look forward to continuing the constructive discussions on the individual elements of the draft law in the Committee on Energy, Supply and Climate Affairs. With these comments, I should like to ask for a favourable treatment of the draft law.

ParlaMint-DK_20171012114539	The debate is open. I take the rapporteurs in the order in which I see the rapporteurs in the Chamber, so I shall start with the rapporteur of the Danish People's Party, Mr. Alex Ahrendtsen. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012114605	Thank you. Well, I can just give the Social Democrat speech, so it makes sense what is being said. Oh! The law on changing the law on building conservation sounds a little boring to some, for me it sounds incredibly exciting. The amendment of the law is, of course, about the possibility of disarming the interior of a number of protected buildings whose inner conservation value has been lost due to remodeling or renovation. Why this bill now? Well, this is because there are about 9,000 listed buildings that have been reviewed in the period 2010-16 by the Danish National Palace and Culture Agency to find out what the state of these buildings really is, and in that process the owners have also been guided. Fortunately, the conclusion was that most of these listed buildings are well preserved and in good condition. There was, however, a small minority with which there were some problems, and that is approx. 500 of these, which are the subject of the draft law. What we then want with the bill is to divide the conservation into an external protection and then allow the owners to change things inside, because the conservation values are simply no longer there. I have looked at what could be permitted changes, and that is for example. to change space size, to move non-bearing partitions, to set up new partitions, to replace the kitchen and bath, to move the location of the kitchen etc. etc. We have seen a number of examples of this in the Ministry where some meetings were held, and that is all very well. It will, of course, make it easier for citizens and businesses to modernise the interior, which no longer meets the conservation conditions. However, I do not think that the legislative proposal is far-reaching enough. In the Danish People's Party we have a number of proposals on what could also have been done, which unfortunately are not included, and here we simply need to clarify whether this change of law can protect buildings whose exterior is worthy of conservation, even if the interior does not comply with the provisions; that possibility was previously provided with A and B-peaces. If we had that opportunity, it would ensure much more historic and conservational buildings. The possibility of protecting the exterior of certain buildings worthy of conservation would make it possible to secure buildings more effectively against the arbitrary use of cultural heritage by local authorities, and it would also make it a little more attractive to have the house protected. As far as I understand the amendment of the law, and here I look at the Minister, it only applies to the approx. 500 buildings among the existing 9,000 already listed buildings. Another thing we would like to look at a little is that buildings worthy of preservation are today a municipal task, and so they do not always solve that equally well, especially when there are interests at stake. I would like to see a proposal on how to tighten up municipal responsibility for buildings worthy of conservation. It would also be good if we could safeguard the environment around the protected buildings by designating protection zones. What is the use of having a protected house if the surroundings completely change their character? We have previously tabled a resolution on this in the Danish People's Party. Whether it should be an A- or a B-peace or whether it should be a protection zone, we must talk about. It is also clear from section 3 of the Building Conservation Act that buildings more than 50 years old can be set for conservation, but then there is a small note that, in exceptional cases, buildings that are younger can be protected if they are of architectural high value, and that can always be discussed. This certainly led to the Viking Ship Museum being protected, and a museum at Bornholm, which was no more than 20 years old before it was protected. I would like the 50-year rule to be general so that you cannot go ahead earlier. It's always good to have a little time before you look at whether buildings are to be protected. Then I would also like to know, in connection with the 1536 rule that all buildings before that date are protected, to find out why just 1536? Is that also true of churches? That is also something we need to look at. Churches before 1536 are automatically protected? But we have to look at all this in the next round, Minister. The Danish People's Party is pleased that the Minister has begun to modernise the Building Conservation Act so small, but we therefore consider it a first small attempt to make the law more up-to-date. The work is, of course, far from complete, but we look forward to further progress and we will ask some questions during the committee procedure. We support the moving bill.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012115130	Thank you to the rapporteur. I see no one with brief comments. The next rapporteur is Hans Andersen, Left. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012115141	Thank you. This bill deals with the rules applicable to changes and renovations of listed buildings. Nowadays, the legislation is in such a way as to seek authorisation for all changes that do not refer to as general maintenance, whether they are changes in the outside or the inner part of the building. Today, as you know, we operate with only one conservation value. With this bill, the government wants to end this. In future, we should not only work with one conservation value, but instead have a differentiated approach to building conservation. Specifically, the bill means that some listed buildings should only be protected on the outside. In other words, if the owner of a listed building, for example, would like to create a new floor, the person should not apply for permission for such a change if the conservation value no longer applies to the inner part of the building. The background to the bill is Slots- og Kulturstyrelsen's recent survey of listed buildings in Denmark • a survey that just showed that some of our listed buildings have great conservation value outside, but to a very small extent or not inside. This supports the mapping that a more differentiated approach to the area makes sense. Current legislation in this area creates both unnecessary bureaucracy and ambiguity in relation to the rules on the conservation of protected buildings, where the conservation values in the interior are no longer present. 500 listed buildings. By adopting this bill, we will, among other things, be voting in favour of it. the owners of the buildings covered are freer and lighten the administrative burden of the authorities in the course of the proceedings. Both make good sense, and on that basis, of course, the Left supports the bill. In conclusion, however, I would like to stress the following: this relaxation is not an expression of the fact that the Left compromises on the preservation of our protected buildings. We need to think about the changes we are making. However, it is the view of the Left that this has been taken into account in the legislative proposal before us. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20171012115420	Thank you, President, and apologies for the delay. The listed buildings that we have in Denmark have a great national and cultural value, from Aalborg Station across Aarhus City Hall to the home of Hans Christian Andersen in Odense. The draft law we are discussing here concerns the amendment of the law on the conservation and conservation of buildings and urban environments. The purpose of the bill is to change the conditions of the buildings which no longer have an inner conservation value. In those buildings the exterior must remain protected, but with the law it will no longer be necessary to apply for permission until the interior of the buildings is rebuilt. Such a change would, firstly, make it easier for the owners of the buildings and, secondly, it would facilitate the proceedings of the authorities, which could of course take time to deal with more urgent cases. So the new proposal thus ensures, we think, a proper balance between the conservation value of a building and the cost of conservation. The proposal is made on the basis of a national review of our protected buildings in Denmark. It has been prepared by Slots- og Kulturstyrelsen and has created an overview of the state of the approx. 9,000 listed buildings we have at home. And it is indeed gratifying that the review concludes that the vast majority of buildings are in good condition. The listed buildings constitute a piece of important Danish cultural history, which we can be proud to preserve and also proud to pass on to posterity. However, the review also concludes that about 500 buildings have only conservation value in the exterior, but internally have been rebuilt or modified to such an extent that it is no longer sensible to maintain a full conservation. That is why it is also sensible to amend the law. The cost of rebuilding a protected building must be the equivalent of the conservation value of the building, both for the owners and for society. That is what the draft law will ensure, and we think that is a positive change in relation to the current legal situation. In addition, it is positive that the bill facilitates both public and private burdens, because the amendment of the law will mean that the Ministry of Culture will have to deal with fewer cases, and since the amendment of the law will provide a better opportunity to rebuild, it will at the same time create a more flexible framework for business and owners. That's a good thing. We have noted that the Architects' School Aarhus and the Royal Danish Academy of Fine Arts School for Architecture, Design and Conservation have had a number of concerns about this. We must take this into account in the work of the committee. In conclusion, I would just like to say that we in the Social Democratic Party greatly appreciate the cultural history of the protected buildings in Denmark, but it is also necessary to ensure a balance between costs, inconvenience and conservation value, which is why we recognise the need for up-to-date changes when necessary. Against this background, the Social Democratic Party can support the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20171012132041	After all, this is an important bill, which is the implementation of a broad political agreement between the government, the Social Democratic Party, the Danish People's Party, the Radical Left and the Socialist People's Party. With this, we will ensure much greater budgetary security for the permanent beneficiaries of the so-called distribution funds. So far, it has been the case that from the funds generated by income from Danske Spil, a percentage has been obtained each year. Thus, it has been uncertain from year to year how much money one would receive. Here we make it a fixed amount received, based on the level of 2013, where there was a high level of distribution, so that the associations can be sure that they can lay their budgets and be sure of the economy they have in the years to come. We do so by dividing the beneficiaries into three groups: group 1, group 2 and group 3, where group 1 and group 2 are guaranteed a fixed amount, and group 3, which is primarily projects with project funds allocated by the individual ministries, will be there, we will regulate if there is less or more in the box. So it is a new model that ensures far greater budgetary security for the vast majority of beneficiaries of distribution funds. It is for the benefit and benefit of the entire Sports and Association Denmark. The second aspect of the draft law is that, when we cannot be sure how much revenue comes from gambling, and there is therefore a risk that for one year there will be less revenue, we will change the distribution of risk. It was before that that those who received the amounts of the sports and association activities carried half the risk while the State carried the other half, and in future it will be the case that the State bears 60%. of the risk and the beneficiaries 40%. So there is greater certainty in the reduction in the amount of games in favour of sport and association in Denmark. That is also positive. In addition, the draft law provides for the liberalisation of horse racing. In future, it will be able to operate independently with five years' support, which will then be phased out over four years, so that horse racing can organise their own games and thus also secure their own income. So here, too, we have created a solution for horse racing, which has faced challenges in recent years. Finally, that agreement is also guaranteed here, and it has been important for the Social Democrats that money has been set aside to combat gambling. For the back of the coin by having a gambling market and also a state-recognised and partly state-owned gambling market is, of course, that we experience people who become game-dependent and sometimes with huge and unhappy consequences for their lives. That is where the state needs to be involved and help, because when we have a state-regulated and partly state-owned gambling market, it is responsible that we take care of those people who need guidance and help. That is why we are very, very pleased that additional funds have also been set aside here to combat gambling. Finally, I should just like to say that the Social Democrats are pleased with the broad consensus we have on this agreement. It is broadly beneficial for Danish cultural, leisure and sports life.

ParlaMint-DK_20171012134658	Thank you for the comments made by the rapporteurs here at first reading today. This is a very important deal. Many people have mentioned the importance of association in Denmark. There is a broad political consensus on this, which is why it is good that we now have security in relation to funding on some very, very important fronts. It was also just noticed by someone that it was a very boring bill. But perhaps the boring legislative proposals are the best, because then it actually shows that we agree that there are good arguments for doing so; then we do not have to smoke so much on each other. But it is certainly positive for the distribution recipients that there is so much agreement as to what the model looks like. It is, after all, the implementation of the political agreement on a change that was concluded in January. In short, the changes are, firstly, a change in the financing model, secondly, a change in the distribution of funds in a fundamental way and, thirdly, a phasing-out of aid for horse racing after a transitional period. I would just like to make a few comments on each of those points. With regard to the financing model, it is changed in such a way that the risk distribution between the State and the recipients of the distribution is changed from a 50-50 to a 60-40 risk sharing by way of deviations from the fixed distribution ceiling, which means that the State will in future help to secure the beneficiaries to a greater extent whose profits and dividends are lower than the fixed ceiling. At the same time, the State will receive a greater share of the profit if profits and dividends exceed the ceiling. At the same time, we adjust the ceiling so that both state and distribution recipients are kept financially harmless, and it has been in demand for a very long time. As far as distribution is concerned, it is now changing and there is the big difference that the fixed recipients will receive a fixed amount from the distribution funds in the future. In the past they received a fixed percentage of the total amount. For many, this has created great uncertainty because the final amount depended on the development of profits and profits from the two gambling companies. So the new distribution model will ensure a greater degree of budgetary security for the permanent beneficiaries, and that is at least the intention of the voluntary association life, which plays a huge role for us in Denmark. Having said that, there has been concern in the course of the hearing, and indeed today, about the funds for Group 3, which are the so-called project funds. Group 3 consists of some project funds located in the various ministries. The idea is, of course, that it is an overflow model, so that group 1 and group 2 are first secured, and if there are additional surpluses then project funds can be set aside. And we have discussed that concern a great deal. The aim of the political agreement was to ensure budgetary security for the main organisations, and it was a premise of the agreement that this could potentially be done at the expense of the Ministry's project funds. However, I would like to say that there are many of us who share that concern, and that is also why we are going to follow it so closely, both in the annual balance sheet, but also in relation to the fact that we have been put in place, that we will have a proper evaluation after the three years. So it is the idea of the evaluation clause that we should see what it looks like in a three-year perspective and how it will also have concrete significance in relation to Group 3 funds. It is also the case that we have agreed that if the forecast in the third quarter of a given year shows that there will be no funding for Group 3, then the model will be discussed. You're gonna call to see what this is, what this is gonna mean, and if there's anything we can do about it. As far as horse racing is concerned, it has also been a very, very long and thorough discussion that we have had. In January, an agreement was reached on the liberalization of gambling, among other things. horse racing, which was implemented by Bill 196, which was adopted in the last session just before the summer holiday. One element of that law is the introduction of a special contribution from gambling providers to horse racing, which will then in future provide support for this sport. That is why we are also proposing in the legislative proposal that after a transitional period of five years, the aid from the distribution will be phased out over four years. The aid during the transitional period corresponds to the level received by horse racing in 2016. I believe that the support will help to ensure that sport can move to a more liberalised market and, once the distribution aid has been phased out, horse racing will only receive support for sport in general. The level of support for sport in general will be fixed in the phase-out and financed by the means of distribution. As part of the agreement on a new model of aid for horse racing, the Fund for horse racing is closed and the aid is distributed directly to sport during the transitional period. I am very confident that the overall model of support for horse racing can help ensure sport in the future too. With those words, I would like to sign for the debate. It has been a long process and a demanding process. There is also a lot of technology in this, but I am glad that there has been such broad support for the proposal as the debate here today also reflects. I look forward to the further examination of the bill and hope for a positive treatment by the Committee on Culture as a whole.

ParlaMint-DK_20171012140749	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no questions. The next rapporteur is Mr Seligman. Hans Andersen, Left. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012140757	Thank you. This bill brings together and clarifies the rights and duties of religions outside the church. The bill contains both a collection and a codification of existing practices concerning religions. At the same time, it contains a number of new initiatives, including supervision of the statutes and economic conditions of the recognised religions. The bill establishes a common terminology between religions, which means that no distinction is made in the future between recognized religions and approved religions. Thus, the approved and recognised religions are treated in the same way as the rights and obligations attached to recognition under the bill. In this context, I would like to stress that the religions which have been approved or recognised under previous approval schemes continue to be regarded as recognised under the terms of the draft law. The draft law contains two points of relaxation in relation to the current authorisation practice. The requirement for a religion to be organized in accordance with the principles of membership democracy and equal treatment does not continue in the draft law. The background is an assessment of whether stricter demands would make it necessary to withdraw the recognition of some traditional and well-established religions. At the same time, the draft law relaxes the membership requirement from 150 to 50 members. The aim is to get more religions to apply for recognition and thereby gain a better overview of religions in Denmark. In May 2016, an agreement was reached on initiatives aimed at religious ministers. These restrictions will be continued in the draft law. At the same time, the bill introduces new supervision of the organization and finances of the recognized religions. This means that recognised religions must regularly submit information about, for example, economics and new statutes to the Register of Religions, which the Ministry of Churches is obliged to establish and operate. The supervision requires recognised religions to prepare and submit annually the most recent annual accounts published on the Commonwealth Register's website. The aim is to improve the transparency of economic conditions and therefore the financial statements must include information on donations. If religious communities do not comply, the bill provides a direct legal basis for withdrawing the recognition of a religion. In conclusion, I would like to mention that the provisions of the Marriage Law on marriage authorisation for persons in other religions are being moved to the law on religions outside the church. We in Denmark have a long and constitutional tradition of freedom of religion, and this bill ensures that the framework for free worship in Denmark remains known. At the same time, the rights and duties of faith societies are made clear and comprehensible. That is why the Left supports the bill. And thank you for the floor.

We believe that freedom of religion must be defended at all times. All people must be allowed to have and carry out their faith and religion, and it is the task of the state to ensure this. But in a true secular society there must be a clear separation of church and state. It is therefore the wish of Enhedslisten that the church of the people should be separated from the state, both economically, legally and administratively, as is the case in, for example, Denmark. Sweden. Our secular basic view also means that in fact, basically, we do not believe that it is at all the task of the state to approve religions. Religious communities should be treated just like all other associations: that they can be created freely without state approval. It also follows, of course, that they should be treated in the same way as other associations. They should not have special economic conditions that they have today, for example. In the form of a large number of deductions, which cost hundreds of millions of kroner a year in total, and of course they should not have transferred public authority tasks. This applies in particular to the legal marriage, which has many civil rights, for example. Inherited consequences. Such an authority should be in our public administration. This does not prevent religions or national churches from having rituals, for example. with marriages and baptisms etc., but the judicial part of a true secular society should lie with public authorities. In relation to the specific law, I would say in relation to the demands of the communities of faith, that it is to such an extent surprising that the government will relax the current demands for religions to be organized in accordance with the principles of membership democracy and equal treatment. It is a clear step backwards, and the Minister may elaborate in his reply which existing religions it is the government believes do not meet those requirements. Then I would like to say a few words about the lack of recognition of life-sight communities. As long as we maintain the idea of a state recognition of religions, it is important for the Enhedslisten that the criteria for recognition follow with time. It is therefore positive that the draft law has to some extent followed this trend when it is no longer necessary to submit creeds or similar canonized texts, but can only submit a description of the teaching tradition. In this way one gets included religions that do not have such descriptions. But we are also concerned with one of the major problems with the bill, namely that we do not open up the possibility of equality between religions and societies of view of life, that is to say, outlooks of life that do not believe in gods or other transcendental powers. But other outlooks are based on e.g. Humanism and the ideas of information, for example in Denmark. represented by Humanist Society, a society of outlooks, which has more members than many recognized religions. Unfortunately, the bill excludes life-sight communities by defining religions on the basis that members must gather together for a belief in powers that are above human beings and natural laws. In doing so, it effectively excludes societies of outlook other than religious ones. This is clearly contrary to the UN recommendations and differs from what several of our neighbouring countries are doing. The UN's then special reporter for Freedom of religion or belief, Heiner Bielefeldt, visited Denmark last year. Then he stressed that by basing the recognition of religious communities on belief in transcendent power, Danish law differs from both European and international human rights, in other words, emphasising that it is contrary to international rules on it. In Norway, there are long-standing equal societies of outlooks and, for example, in Norway. granted them the right to marry. In 2016, there were more than 700 humanistic marriages in Norway. The same is true in Iceland, where I was here this weekend. It is therefore very regrettable that the government, with the law, insists on this discrimination against different outlooks of life. We will table an amendment in the Enhedslisten which will ensure that societies of outlook for life are treated in the same way as religious religions, and we will ask the Minister to explain the position of international human rights on the bill's demand for belief in powers that are above human beings and natural laws. Our position on the legislative proposal will depend on this issue.

ParlaMint-DK_20171012151910	We'll resume the meeting. The debate is open. Mrs Karin Gaardsted, the Social Democratic Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012151940	Thank you for that, President. With the proposal we are dealing with here, parts of the insurance distribution directive are implemented in Danish law. It is consumer protection in the field of insurance that is in focus. It zooms into consumer needs, guarantees equal protection, regardless of who is buying insurance, and introduces equal treatment between the different actors in the insurance sector, by ensuring that everyone has to comply with the same rules. That makes sense in every way. The aim of the Insurance Distribution Directive is to modernise and harmonise the rules of the European Union so that all consumers in the European Union receive the same basic protection when buying insurance. The aim is to implement directives closely, and therefore it also seems meaningful to repeal the existing law on insurance mediation when the insurance distribution directive is implemented and to draw up a completely new law on insurance mediation on the basis of the directive. At the same time, we also think it is good that a number of the provisions of the law on insurance mediation should be maintained in order to ensure the independence of insurance brokers, for example, that they will be subject to special requirements for the organisation of their business and be subject to an absolute commission ban. As this is a brand new law we are dealing with here, of course the proposal comes all the way around. I would therefore just like to dwell on a few points of impact. We think it is very good that there should be an increase in consumer protection in this area, and this is the case, among other things. for the simple reason that more market players will need to be authorised in the future and that they will therefore be subject to supervision by the FSA. This means that in the future, much more players will be subject to the requirement that they comply with the permit requirements. They must meet greater demands on their behaviour in the form of good practice rules, remuneration rules and competence requirements. This is all good for the consumer, but also healthy for competition in this area. As more actors are to be monitored, we will be working on whether there are enough resources now to enable the FSA to carry out the extended task that is now being entrusted to them. It is also proposed that operators should be required to provide standardised documents on the key elements of their insurance products. This should give consumers a better overview and allow them to easily compare insurance. In this way, it will be easier for consumers to make an informed and conscious choice of insurance. That is also positive. It is also worth noting that in future all insurance intermediaries will have to make a demand and demand analysis of their customers so that customers will only be offered products they need. That is, no dog insurance for cat owners or car insurance for passionate cyclists without a car. It must also help consumers to have the same information and protection wherever they buy their insurance and from whom they buy their insurance. It is all good for the consumer, but here we are at a point where we see some challenges in terms of how this can be put into practice at all, and we hope to have it clarified during committee work. The draft law also ensures that rules on the competence of insurance and reinsurance intermediaries can be issued. A similar authorisation is provided for in the Law on Financial Activities for Insurance Companies. This means that employees in the industry provide competent advice and guidance and contribute to a high level of professionalism in the industry. Finally, we welcome the fact that we note that the good measures on the whistleblower scheme, implemented under the auspices of the FSA in the context of banking package VI, are being continued. All in all, this is a sensible proposal, which also seems to be a directive-close proposal. We look forward to the work of the committee, where, as I have said, the Social Democrats will drill into whether the FSA resource is geared to it, when more players are coming in that needs to be monitored. And since we are now dealing with a whole new law, there may of course also be a need to look a little further into the consultation answers.

ParlaMint-DK_20171012153548	Thank you. With the bill, the Government and the Folketing implement parts of the Insurance Distribution Directive. ParlaMint-DK_20171012154220	Thank you to the rapporteur. Then it's the Minister of Business.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012154224	Thank you for your comments and for the generally positive reception of the draft law. After all, we are introducing a new law on insurance mediation, and the law transposes parts of the insurance distribution directive into Danish law. The aim of the directive is to create a level playing field between all the different players who can distribute insurance. This is done by ensuring that all aspects of the distribution of insurance must comply with the same rules, thereby ensuring the same protection for consumers. At the same time, the directive sets a high level of consumer protection and requires companies to take into account the interests and needs of customers from the time they develop new insurance products. With the bill, we will ensure uniform protection for Danish consumers regardless of whether they buy their insurance from an insurance company, from an agent or from another insurance company. The draft law will improve consumer protection by allowing or registering several market players and then being subject to supervision by the FSA. And that is where there is a small discrepancy with the Consumer Council, which I hear one of the rapporteurs listens to a great deal. The Consumer Council is not in general opposed to the proposal, but states that they would like the area to be extended, in other words to have more supervision of ancillary insurance intermediaries. It is our view that we stick to the directive. Of course, we will keep monitoring, including small businesses, and from the FSA you can obtain information for the purposes of supervision and then go on inspection if it is. So that is where there is a small niche difference between the Consumer Council and our proposal now here. But generally speaking, this proposal is consumer protection. The proposal also lays down requirements for the actors, which means that we continue requirements for suitability and liability for what we also call "fit & property" to the managers of the covered insurance intermediaries. The requirements must strengthen the management of companies and, together with the other regulation of these companies, contribute to ensuring sound and efficient operation. As a new feature, it is proposed that individual employees of so-called ancillary insurance intermediaries should be subject to honesty requirements. It is companies which distribute insurance as a supplement to their main performance, e.g. white goods companies selling insurance to their refrigerators etc. The requirement shall ensure that persons have a good reputation when they have overall responsibility for the distribution of ancillary insurance. The proposal also contains new rules on the remuneration of ancillary insurance intermediaries. Specifically, no remuneration may be paid in a way that encourages the person selling an insurance product to recommend to the customer a product that is not the best for the customer. In addition, greater demands will be made on the behaviour of distributors in the form of rules of good practice and requirements for specific competences. This will be done by issuing notices on this subject, which are based on the draft law. It is also a matter of the insurance intermediaries mentioned by the rapporteur of the Danish People's Party. We do not want to make any further demands on them, like the Consumer Council, but we insist on the demands that the directive must make. And the competence requirements will be the same for the small brokers as for the big brokers, and it is not the case that you have to attend courses and all sorts of other things, but you have to be able to pass the same tests. The point of the Danish People's Party is of course relevant to ensuring that there is still a wide variation of brokers. But the requirements, the requirements of competence, must be the same, whether you are a small broker or a big broker. It just shouldn't be the case that you keep sanding up in different courses and so on. These very permits are not given in the bill itself, but are subsequently given in a notice which, of course, comes into consultation, i.e.. It is the competence requirements that will be consulted, including in this regard. No, the competence requirements are in the legislative proposal itself, but the authorisations must also be discussed in this respect. Competence requirements must help to support a high level of professionalism in the sector, and competence requirements will help to increase customer confidence in the competence of individual employees to provide advice and guidance. The rules ensure this, among other things. also that consumers will have a better overview and an opportunity to compare non-life insurance products. This is done by requiring the provision of a standardised document containing the essential characteristics of the individual insurance. In addition, all insurance distributors must comply with new rules to ensure that customers are offered only products suitable for the individual. In order to maintain the status of insurance brokers on the market as independent intermediaries, the draft law contains a chapter on additional requirements for independent insurance intermediaries which essentially continue to apply the rules in force. The rules must include: ensure that independent insurance intermediaries are solely responsible for the interests of customers. In those words, I look forward to the discussion of the draft law in committee.

There may be all kinds of good reasons for this, but in some areas it might be even more interesting to hear what was actually said in such a technical review of such a complicated legislative proposal as this. It is not necessarily complicated into the individual elements, but there are many things at stake, and if you hear that there are individual parties voting against one and the other in such a proposal, then it can be just as individual rapporteurs have said, one element out of many. That understanding could be very interesting, so I would like to ask for a technical review. In general, I am very much in favour of the vast majority of elements of the proposal, but will not take a unified position yet.

ParlaMint-DK_20171012162611	Thank you. Then the next speaker is the Minister of Business. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012162622	Thank you. And thank you for the comments on the bill amending the cvr law and various other laws. I would like to begin by stating that, as a starting point, we will always be advocates of openness about the ownership and management relationships of Danish companies. Transparency is important for the sake of the confidence of the outside world in our companies, and it contributes to legal certainty for business partners and creditors. There are, however, cases in which the concern of private individuals to protect their information must prevail. In the government, we think it is appropriate to adjust the rules for the publication of names and private addresses in the CVR, so that we take more account of the wishes of private individuals to protect their information. I think that the proposal has struck a very good balance between the need for transparency on the one hand and the need to protect private information on the other. As it is today, a number of information about companies is published in the Central Business Register, CVR. In addition to information about the industry, address and number of employees, you can also find information about names and private addresses for e.g. Directors, board members and owners. It is the Government's view that there may be entirely legitimate reasons why you do not want your private address published in the CVR. It can, for example, be done. be persons who, as part of their profession, are in contact with mentally ill or criminals. It may also be people who want address protection for private reasons, for example for the sake of violent ex-partners etc. We therefore suggest that you can have address protection in CVR if you register address protection via citizen.dk in the Central Personal Register. We also propose that private addresses be updated and published for up to three years after the person was last associated with a company's CVR. Today, the address is updated for 10 years, but published forever. We do so because we do not believe that the continuous updating of people's addresses for 10 years after they have closed their business is proportionate to the purpose of making personal data available to creditors, for example. In the same way, we propose that in future names are only updated in the CVR for 3 years compared to 10 years today. However, name details will continue to appear in the CVR forever, even if their update has stopped. The Danish Business Authority will continue to register address information in the Danish Agency's IT systems, regardless of whether the information is protected and therefore not published. Similarly, public authorities will continue to have access to name and address information, whether published or not, in contact with the Danish Business Authority. Private persons wishing to obtain a protected address must in future contact a municipality. He or she must be able to prove that he or she has a due claim for the person or another legal interest in obtaining the address. I propose that in a few years' time we look at the impact of the proposed rules, including the extent of protected addresses. Here it would be natural to consider the extent to which the changes have led to an increase in the number of requests to municipalities for the provision of protected addresses. In addition, this proposal proposes simplifying the rules on the protection of advertising of companies. This means, among other things, that persons who collect data on advertising protected companies via the CVR will not have to fill in a written declaration in future that they will respect the company's desire not to be contacted. We also propose that, in future, the ban on recourse to advertising-protected companies only covers direct marketing, where it now covers all requests. We do so because the rules in force seem unintended, as they prevent a potential partner or customer from making legal representations to a protected company. It is important to stress that the changes do not weaken the protection of advertising by companies. The bill also contains a few minor amendments to the auditor's law and to the catering law. Then the Socialist rapporteur mentioned why neither children nor grandchildren are involved in this. There is our assessment that the young person will be protected more when the person responsible for ensuring safe working conditions is a family member. It is therefore quite crucial to say that it is not a general weakening of it; it is just a question of saying that there are, of course, other conditions, if it is a family member, it is about it will be very natural. Sir. Pelle Dragsted from Enhedslisten also asks why we bring something about the Audit Committee. In other words, it is a requirement under EU rules that directors, who are also part of the company's management board, should not be a member of the audit committee. This requirement has been in force since the statutory auditor's act in 2018, but fell by mistake in relation to the legal amendment in 2016. Therefore, the proposal here is to bring the provision into line with EU rules and the rules previously in force in the auditor's law. The alternative rapporteur, René Gade, is in principle opposed to these major package proposals. So was I when I was in opposition. Now there are some things that need to be resolved, but I think it is appropriate, and I respect, of course, that you want to have a technical review of it. We would very much like to invite this to the Department of Business so that we can sort of address the individual themes one by one. We would very much like to invite both Mr. René Gade and others to the Department of Business.

ParlaMint-DK_20171012165209	You're welcome. There are no brief comments, so the next speaker is the rapporteur on the left, Mr President. Torsten Schack Pedersen. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012165218	After all, financial regulation is important for banks and mortgage banks and investment funds and all sorts of other companies in the financial sector, because it plays a quite central role in the Danish economy and for our prosperity. The conditions for saving and investing and borrowing money are crucial to the conditions for growth that businesses have and the opportunities that we as citizens have to realise our economic aspirations. That is why it is, of course, a question of having a healthy and well-functioning financial sector in which we can have confidence. This requires constant regulation of the sector so that we avoid future financial crises. This bill basically ensures that the regulation of the financial sector is up to date and that it will also improve consumer and investor protection in the financial field. This is a long draft law of 200 pages, so I would just like to highlight some of the elements, the central ones which have also been mentioned by the two previous rapporteurs, in particular the proposal that the FSA should be given the opportunity to draw up and publish guidelines for good practice in the field of consumer affairs. This means that the FSA, in cooperation with representatives of the financial sector and consumers, must agree on the guidelines and thus create a common understanding of the treatment of consumers using services in the financial sector. The draft law also gives the FSA a better opportunity to deal with individual citizens' complaints about financial activities. The proposal ensures that in future the FSA may ask a financial undertaking to explain the behaviour they have shown to the customer in a complaint. The extended powers of the FSA strengthen consumer protection in cases where financial companies are involved and where the consumer believes that things have not been done properly. In addition, it is also a question of the principle of portability, which has also been mentioned by the previous rapporteurs, who have to safeguard the pension rights of EU citizens moving across borders. This means that employees moving to another EU-EEA country will be able to earn a pension outside the Danish borders. Similarly, employees moving from an EU-EEA country can earn a pension and they can make sure that any previous pensions can remain resting. It helps to support the fact that you as an employee will not be squeezed if you work across borders. In addition, the proposal also strengthens the exchange of information between the institution and the investor on investment products. This is the implementation of the so-called PRIIP regulation, which aims to give investors easier access to information on investment products. In this way, greater transparency and a better decision-making basis are created for investors, and this significantly strengthens investor protection. And stronger investor protection should, of course, lead to more investment. Similarly, the benchmark controls of the Benchmark Regulation are also carried out so that they become more true. The price of financial instruments is today set according to so-called benchmark, and therefore it is important that they are accurately calculated and that they are reliable; it again ensures good investor protection. And by imposing controls and sanctions for the processing of benchmarks, the proposal ensures that the financial sector uses more robust and fair-tuning benchmarks. As I said, these were some of the elements of the bill. There are also changes around SIFI, part will say how we calculate which institutions are so-called SIFI, i.e. system important financial institutions, which are adapted to the reality that whether it is a Danish financial institution or a branch of a foreign company, it is of course important for the size of the financial sector in Denmark. It gives a more accurate picture. This is a great but quite sensible change, which we support from the Left.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012165634	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no brief comments. The next rapporteur is Mr Seligman. Pelle Dragsted, Enhedslisten. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012165643	Thank you, President or Chairwoman, or whatever you say. After all, this is another one in a long series of proposals that we have seen in these years, which are about regulating the financial sector. It is complicated matter, and one might say that these proposals and their complex nature represent a very good picture of the extent to which our financial sector has developed into a bit of a wilderness. One might wonder whether, instead of trying to regulate the wilderness, we should clear it up a little and simplify things slightly. Maybe there's a little too many creative products out there. That is actually what I would like to say a little about, because the other rapporteurs have already mentioned many of the concrete elements. In the Enhedslisten, we warmly support the strict regulation of the financial sector. It is a sector of enormous importance to society, and it can be said that a few years ago the financial crisis clearly showed that there is no reason to have the great confidence that the private financial sector itself takes responsibility for safeguarding its citizens and society against the harmful effects of financial crises. On the contrary, they showed a lack of responsibility. Basically, I just have to say that we do not think that the regulation that has been introduced since the crisis is sufficient at all. We still have some financial giants that are so big that if they go down, they can pull down the whole economy. It is totally untenable that private groups can have that influence on our society and on the security of our citizens. That is why we basically believe that much more far-reaching reforms are needed, such as: This involves the division of financial giants or superpowers, so that investment banks, banking, mortgage and insurance are again divided into separate groups, as we had legislation on just a few decades ago. During the 1980s and 1990s, we were part of a trend of deregulating the financial sector. It couldn't be free enough, and the state couldn't interfere a little enough, so everything would go well, we were told. It just did not do so, and the sector to which we left enormous responsibility showed that it could not fulfil that responsibility at all. That is why we need to roll back deregulation and liberalisation. This concentration of ownership by a few giant corporations is not only a threat to financial stability, it is also a threat to customers. We see the mortgage sector as the best example right now, how ordinary Danes are used as economic milking machines, where you set up fees and contributions rates over and over again and in this way have the trunk down in the pockets of home owners, borrowers and bank customers, while paying huge billions of dollars to distant owners. So basically, we believe that a completely new financial system is needed. Now we saw that Deutsche Bank said the other day that the next financial crisis is just around the corner. Oh, is it? Then you turn to the next page of the paper and find that we can also discuss a bit of burka and that is much more important than if we are heading for a new financial crisis! We have a small proposal that regulates some small corners, and there are, as I see it, both positive and negative elements in the proposal. However, the proposal does not fundamentally upset the problems we have with the fact that very few large financial institutions have much power over our economy and therefore over ordinary people's everyday lives. If I have to point out something that we think is sensible, it is that you tighten up the rules for what you call variable pay, i.e. how much you have to pay in the form of these fantastic bonus schemes that we have seen lately, among other things. in Nets. I think most of us would like similar bonus schemes, but few hardworking Danes cut a bonus of 600 million. kr. for a few years of faithful service. So it's fine that it gets tightened up. The amendments to the SIFI rules also seem to us to be sensible at first sight. Perhaps it would be interesting to clarify the extent to which institutions will then fall outside in the future. On the other hand, it also sounds as if the softening that is, perhaps, reasonable enough in relation to the fact that when you pay off your loan free of charge, it should be done immediately and not after, for example, after. 5 years. But we will also take a closer look at this in committee. Have I talked over time? Well, then I'll stop here. As I said, the Enhedslisten will make a final decision on the proposal later. We believe that far more far-reaching reforms are needed to safeguard citizens against both fee vultures and new financial crises. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20171012173402	Thank you, President. I have taken this matter on behalf of our tax rapporteur, Mr. Jesper Petersen, who has no opportunity to be here today. This bill is, of course, part of the implementation of the political agreement of 22 March 2017 between the government, the Social Democratic Party, the Danish People's Party, the Radical Left and the SF on the development of the North Sea and, as those who have been following the debate in the Chamber since this morning will know, I was also rapporteur on L 42, which is the second part of the implementation of this agreement, and therefore some of what I am saying will be identical to what I said this morning. So to those who have heard it once, I apologise in advance. For the Social Democratic Party, it has been important as a party to the agreement to secure the many jobs that have been at stake in the North Sea. Activities in the North Sea employ thousands of Danes. With the agreement on the reconstruction of the Tyra field, we have secured a central infrastructure for activities in the North Sea and, therefore, also the many jobs that have been at risk of disappearing. Social democracy ensured that the financing of the agreement was fair and socially balanced, as opposed to the government's original funding proposal. Without an agreement, there would be less money available to secure and develop our welfare societies. It is now worth rebuilding the field in the North Sea, while the state, in turn, gets a share in the surplus that may come. We are satisfied with that. We are so pleased that the agreement meets support across the political parties in the Folketing. So little about the concrete bill. It is proposed to introduce an investment window for hydrocarbon producers in the period 2017-2025. The window implies an increase in the depreciation and hydrocarbon allowance rates for investments in production platforms. In addition, an additional tax is proposed, which means, in whole or in part, that producers have to pay back the tax relief in the case of high oil prices. Finally, it is proposed that the advance VAT system should be abolished, so that the proceeds from it are included in the financing of the tax reliefs. Overall, the draft law aims to improve the possibilities for cooperation in the North Sea and ensure that infrastructure is used in the most socio-economically appropriate way. And this is a proposal which, of course, the Social Democratic Party can support, as long as we are part of the agreement.

ParlaMint-DK_20171012175803	Well, then we'll thank Mr. Knox. Dennis Flydtkjær. The next is Mrs Louise Schack Elholm, Left. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012175811	Thank you. And thank you for the floor. The draft law, as several of my predecessors, the rapporteurs of the other parties, have said, translates part of the political agreement on the development of the North Sea. It was concluded between the government, the Social Democratic Party, the Danish People's Party, the Radical Left and the Socialist People's Party in March this year. The overall purpose of this bill is to create an investment room to renovate the infrastructure currently used in the production of oil and gas in the North Sea, including the Tyrafelt plant, which accounts for most of the oil and gas production in Denmark. With the proposal, we are introducing a reduction in the taxation of hydrocarbons during the period 2017-2025. It creates space for an investment in the very worn-out infrastructure and allows us also to extract resources from the North Sea in the future. Specifically, this means that, in a window covering the period 2017-2025, hydrocarbon producers must be able to choose to calculate the hydrocarbon income under a scheme which involves an increase in the depreciation and hydrocarbon allowance rates for investments in production platforms, etc. and an advance on the date of entry into service of the dates of payment in relation to such depreciation and deductions. In addition, an additional tax is introduced, the purpose of which is to allow the hydrocarbon producers who have chosen to make use of the scheme to pay back all or part of the tax relief in the event of high oil prices. Due to the repayment of the tax relief by means of an additional tax, it would be optional for the hydrocarbon tax payers to make use of the scheme. The proposal will provide industry with the necessary tax incentives to ensure improved conditions for investment in the production of oil and gas in the North Sea. Improving production conditions in the North Sea will strengthen companies in the oil and gas industry for the benefit of all Danish users. That is why the Left supports the bill.
ParlaMint-DK_20171012180011	Thank you. There is one question from Mr. Rune Lund, Enhedslisten. Here you go.

A tax clause of this kind is something that is usually seen only in weak states, not in countries such as Denmark.
